<p align="center"><b>PART I</b></p>

<p></p>

<p><b></b></p>

<p><a name="Item_1"></a><b>ITEM 1.&nbsp;&nbsp; BUSINESS</b></p>

<p></p>

<p><b>Overview</b></p>

<p></p>

<p>Intergraph Corporation ("Intergraph" or the "Company") is a leading global supplier of spatial information management software.&nbsp; Intergraph's core mission is to enable businesses and governments to make better and faster operational decisions, and through
software and services, help its customers organize vast amounts of complex data into understandable visual representations.&nbsp; Intergraph's technology enables customers to create intelligent maps, manage assets and infrastructure, build and better manage plants
and ships, and dispatch, command, and control emergency services to those in need.&nbsp; Founded in 1969, the Company was a pioneer of computer graphics software and services in the commercial and government sectors and has delivered numerous innovations in
interactive graphics solutions.&nbsp; The Company's divisions offer software, professional services, and maintenance solutions to satisfy engineering, design, modeling, analysis, mapping, and information technology ("IT") needs.&nbsp; Products and services are sold through industry-focused
direct and indirect sales channels worldwide.&nbsp; Approximately 51% of revenues are generated outside the U.S., primarily in Europe (31%) and Asia Pacific (11%).&nbsp;</p>

<p></p>

<p>Over the past several years, the Company has taken significant measures to transform itself from a hardware provider to a company focused on software and services.&nbsp; These measures included extensive reductions in its workforce and the sale of several non-core
business units and assets.&nbsp; In fourth quarter 1999, the Company exited the personal computer and generic server businesses, and in third quarter 2000, it ceased the development and design of most of its remaining hardware products.&nbsp; These actions
contributed to the Company's return to profitability in 2001. The Company's profitability continued in 2002 and 2003 although income from operations as a percentage of revenues was less than 3% in each of these years.&nbsp; In 2004, operating income increased as a
percentage of revenue to 6.2% as a result of higher gross margins and general and administrative expense reductions from restructuring initiatives.&nbsp;&nbsp;</p>

<p></p>

<p>The Company completed a strategic planning process in late July 2004 ("Strategic Plan").&nbsp; The results of the process are intended to serve as the foundation for the Company's strategy as it pursues growth.&nbsp; The Company's strategy emphasizes leveraging
its existing assets and capabilities, identifying and capitalizing on attractive market opportunities, exiting non-core businesses, and making prudent investments in new products and acquisitions.&nbsp; As a result of this planning process, the Company identified
three positive themes in the business that Intergraph intends to leverage over the coming years and identified several challenges it must address.</p>

<p></p>

<p>First, the Company believes that Intergraph is more relevant today in the world than it was five years ago.&nbsp; The current global environment is characterized by heightened security concerns, rising demand for energy and infrastructure, and increased adoption
and awareness of geospatial and location-aware applications.&nbsp; &nbsp;</p>

<p></p>

<p>Second, the Company believes its core addressable market opportunities are growing and present the Company with opportunities to seek growth.&nbsp; The Company's strategy is to pursue opportunities in its markets through organic growth and investments in research
and development.&nbsp; The Company will also consider acquiring companies or technologies, particularly within the security or process industry markets.</p>

<p></p>

<p>Third, the Company believes its customers recognize the commitment that the Company has made to the markets it serves.&nbsp; As a result, the Company has customer relationships that in many cases span decades.&nbsp; The Company intends to extend these durable
customer relationships over the coming years by bringing additional products to market organically, as well as seeking to acquire companies and/or product offerings that have appeal to the Company's customer base.</p>

<p></p>

<p>The Company has also identified some areas for improvement.&nbsp; First, the Company believes it sells too little software for its current level of development spending.&nbsp;&nbsp; The Company believes the combination of this revenue mix and the difference in
gross margins between software sales and services projects contributes to the Company's relatively lower operating performance.&nbsp; As part of its efforts to improve performance, the Company is seeking to increase the software related components of its revenue and
to improve the efficiency of its research and development efforts by emphasizing expected returns, strategic importance, and innovation.&nbsp;</p>

<p></p>

<p>Second, the Company believes its former organizational structure of four separate business units impeded intercompany cross-selling and synergies. The Company believes that certain of its sales and marketing efforts were too business-unit focused and failed to
take advantage of the complete set of capabilities that Intergraph has to offer to the marketplace.&nbsp; The Company is seeking to emphasize cross-selling among its divisions, teamwork, and productivity.&nbsp; For example, the newly combined Security Government
&amp; Infrastructure ("SG&amp;I") division has allowed the Company to fuse its spatial and security products to provide a differentiated solution to address the heightened demand for global security and critical infrastructure protection projects.&nbsp;</p>

<p></p>

<p>Third, the Company believes its general and administrative costs are too high given its goal to improve operating performance.&nbsp; As a result, the Company is working to improve its general and administrative processes in an effort to reduce costs.&nbsp; The
Company's ability to execute its strategies and improve its operating results is subject to known and unknown risks and uncertainties.&nbsp;</p>

<p></p>

<p>In April 2005, consistent with its Strategic Plan, the Company announced a realignment of its organizational structure and streamlined its global operations from four to two divisions (the "Reorganization") -- Process, Power &amp; Marine ("PP&amp;M") and the newly
formed SG&amp;I.&nbsp; Intergraph's SG&amp;I division consists of core operations that previously existed in Intergraph Mapping and Geospatial Solutions, Intergraph Solutions Group, and Intergraph Public Safety.&nbsp; The organizational realignment is intended to:
(1) improve the customer focus and responsiveness of the Company; (2) facilitate revenue growth by better leveraging the Company's full range of technology and services; (3) enhance the Company's development capabilities and ability to deliver innovative solutions to
its target markets; and (4) reduce the overall cost structure of the Company.&nbsp; In total, the Company has eliminated approximately 215 positions and reported restructuring charges of $10.0 million related to the Reorganization in the second through fourth
quarters of 2005. The Company expects the Reorganization to be completed by the end of the second quarter of 2006 and estimates that total restructuring charges for the remainder of the Reorganization will be an additional $9.0 - $11.0 million in the first half of
2006.&nbsp; The Company estimates that the entire Reorganization will generate gross expense savings on an annual basis in the range of $23 - $28 million.&nbsp; As part of the Company's transformation effort and in conjunction with its Strategic Plan, Intergraph
plans to invest a portion of the gross expense savings generated by the Reorganization into research and development for core product upgrades, IT and system improvements, expansion of sales channels, and targeted growth opportunities where
the Company believes it has differentiated capabilities.&nbsp;</p>

<p></p>

<p>In late fiscal year 2003, the Company outlined its intention to improve operating margins (income from operations divided by revenue) to levels more in line with its peer groups.&nbsp; While primarily focused on operating margins, management also monitors revenue,
gross margins, and operating expenses for each division and the Company as a whole.&nbsp; The Company anticipates modest revenue growth and slight improvements in gross margins for 2006.&nbsp; This revenue and margin growth is expected to be partially offset by
increased operating expenses, including significant restructuring charges noted above, resulting in higher income from operations than in 2005.&nbsp; As part of restructuring, and similar to the last two years, the Company anticipates that income from operations will
grow at rates greater than revenue.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</p>

<p></p>

<p>Most of the Company's income before taxes in the last five years originated from $768.4 million of intellectual property ("IP") income, net, $32.2 million of gains on sales of non-core assets, and $33.3 million of interest income.&nbsp; In order to provide
shareholders with the ability to better understand and monitor operating performance and improvement, all income and expenses associated with the IP portfolio, including legal expenses, are classified and reported in the "Other income (expense), net" section of the
income statement.&nbsp; The Company carries minimal debt of $771,000 (primarily for a capital lease) and has repurchased 25.8 million shares of its common stock for approximately $640.6 million over the last five years before the settlement of the Company's current
Accelerated Stock Buyback ("ASB") program, which will end on March 21, 2006.&nbsp;</p>

<p></p>

<p>The Company believes its addressable end markets are large and growing at 5-10% per annum and is committed to improving its sales and marketing execution in an effort to increase its revenue growth rate over time.&nbsp; Management monitors the orders and backlog metrics for
systems and services to manage its sales efforts.&nbsp; Orders represent the value of contracts sold in a period excluding maintenance contracts and multi-year contracts that require fiscal funding.&nbsp; Backlog represents the remaining value of orders that have yet
to be recognized as revenue at a point in time.&nbsp; Many orders received by the Company relate to long-term projects that require the Company to recognize revenue based on percentage of completion, product delivery, and/or acceptance.&nbsp; Therefore, the time
between order acceptance and complete revenue recognition can be as long as 12-24 months.&nbsp; Increasingly, the Company is being asked by customers to integrate its products with third party products, or to add additional custom functionality.&nbsp; The uncertainty
in timing from backlog to revenue recognition may cause non-linear fluctuations or variations in revenue.&nbsp; Orders for 2005 were $468.8 million reflecting an increase of 11% over orders in 2004.&nbsp; Backlog increased to $245.5 million at December 31, 2005
compared to $202.2 million at December 31, 2004, reflecting a 21% increase.</p>

<p></p>

<p>Fluctuations of the U.S. dollar in international markets could have a significant impact on the Company's results of operations.&nbsp; In 2005, the Company estimates that the weakening of the U.S. dollar in the Company's international markets, primarily in Europe,
positively impacted revenue by approximately 0.6%, negatively impacted operating expenses by approximately 0.5%, and improved the Company's net income by approximately $0.03 per diluted share in comparison to 2004.&nbsp; A strengthening U.S. dollar could materially
adversely affect the Company's future results of operations.</p>

<p></p>

<p>In addition, the Company owns various IP as a result of its research and design efforts, much of which was retained from hardware operations previously exited by the Company.&nbsp; The IP is used in a variety of industries, including computers, consumer
electronics, telecommunications, and electronics design.&nbsp; In order to protect shareholder interests, the Company has defended the value of its IP portfolio through licensing and litigation.&nbsp; The Company may or may not elect to pursue additional IP
litigation in the future.&nbsp; In the first quarter of 2005, the Company announced that it had entered into a global settlement agreement covering all pending patent litigation with Hewlett-Packard Co.<sup>TM</sup> ("HP").&nbsp; Under the terms of the agreement, HP
paid Intergraph $141.1 million in cash.&nbsp; The terms provided for the dismissal of all pending legal actions between Intergraph and HP as well as cross-licenses to the other party's patent portfolio.&nbsp; The settlement with HP resolved all of the Company's
outstanding patent litigation for the first time in over seven years.&nbsp; In third quarter 2005, Intergraph announced a patent license agreement with Fujitsu.&nbsp; Under the terms of the agreement, Fujitsu agreed to a one-time upfront royalty payment of $9.8
million and obtained a fully paid-up worldwide license to certain Intergraph Clipper patents for specified Fujitsu products. While IP enforcement is inherently uncertain, Intergraph will continue to consider various strategies to protect and defend the Company's
IP.&nbsp; For the year ended December 31, 2005, Intergraph reported $140.7 million of pre-tax intellectual property income, net of all fees and expenses.</p>

<p></p>

<p>On March 22, 2005, the Company repurchased approximately 5.4 million shares from Goldman, Sachs &amp; Co. ("Goldman Sachs") in a private transaction in connection with an ASB program. The shares were repurchased for an upfront payment of approximately $150.3
million or $27.74 per share, subject to a market price adjustment provision based on the volume weighted average market trading price over the period from May 2, 2005 through March 21, 2006. The Company's volume weighted average trading price has been approximately
$41.10 per share from May 2, 2005 through March 3, 2006.&nbsp; If the Company's share price continues to trade in the range of $36.00 to $39.00 per share throughout the remainder of the ASB period, then the volume weighted average trading price would be approximately
$40.80 to $41.00 per share, and if Intergraph were to settle in cash, the Company would make an additional payment of approximately $70.5 to $72 million to Goldman Sachs, or if Intergraph were to settle in shares, it would issue approximately 1.8 to 2.0 million
shares to Goldman Sachs. The ultimate amount of any additional cash payment or share settlement may vary substantially depending on the final volume weighted average price of the common stock for the applicable period.</p>

<p></p>

<p><b>Financial Information About Segments</b></p>

<p><b></b></p>

<p>See Note 15 of Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements in this Form 10-K ("Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements") for financial information presented by segment.</p>

<p></p>

<p><b>Business Segments</b></p>

<p><b></b></p>

<p>The Company's current operations are divided into two business segments along with a Corporate oversight function ("Corporate").&nbsp; The Company's business segments are PP&amp;M and SG&amp;I.&nbsp; Each business is discussed in further detail below.&nbsp; For
additional information regarding the Company's business segments, including financial information for 2005, see Management's Discussion and Analysis of Financial Condition and Results of Operations ("MD&amp;A") and Note 15 of Notes to Consolidated Financial
Statements.</p>

<p></p>

<p><b><i>PP&amp;M:</i></b>&nbsp; PP&amp;M supplies software and services to the process, power, offshore petroleum, and marine industries, including shipbuilding.&nbsp; The segment focuses on integrated lifecycle engineering software and services for design and
information management, with an emphasis on engineering information, materials and procurement management, and the linkage of engineering and business systems.</p>

<p></p>

<p>For more than 25 years, engineering, procurement, and construction contractors and facility owner/operators have used PP&amp;M's software and services to design, construct, operate, and maintain facilities for petrochemical, chemical, and pharmaceutical process
manufacturing; food and beverage; marine oil and gas; nuclear, coal, and gas-fired power generation; pulp and paper; commercial shipbuilding; and mining industries.&nbsp; PP&amp;M software is used to design and build some of the world's critical infrastructure.&nbsp;
PP&amp;M competes in the software market for plant creation, life cycle management, and operations and maintenance primarily against AVEVA Group plc ("AVEVA") and Bentley Systems, Inc. ("BSI") (an approximately 28% basic or 25% fully diluted, owned affiliate of the Company).&nbsp; PP&amp;M also
competes against AVEVA in the offshore platform and shipbuilding software market.&nbsp; Positive factors potentially affecting competition in 2006 include rising global demand for energy, the continued acceptance of SmartPlant<sup>&reg;</sup>enterprise software,
increased software lease revenue, and strong demand for SmartPlant<sup>&reg;</sup> software for plant information management, instrumentation engineering, and materials and procurement management.&nbsp; Potential negative factors include worldwide uncertainties
arising from the sustainability of economic recovery and growth, as well as industry conditions affecting funding of customers' capital projects, such as fluctuations in energy and chemical prices.</p>

<p></p>

<p>The segment's most prominent brands include PDS<sup>&trade;</sup> (Plant Design System), SmartPlant<sup>&reg;</sup>, MARIAN<sup>&reg;</sup> and IntelliShip<sup>&reg;</sup>.&nbsp; SmartPlant<sup>&reg;</sup> 3D Version 6 began shipping in July 2005, and new software
releases continue to be delivered across the SmartPlant<sup>&reg;</sup> enterprise suite, including releases for instrumentation, electrical, visualization, materials management, information management, and drawing generation applications.&nbsp; Strategic
acquisitions in 2005 included EYECAD 3D software from Asahi Kasei Engineering Corp., expanding PP&amp;M's Asian market presence.&nbsp; EYECAD 3D is the leading plant design solution among Japan's domestic engineering companies who have used EYECAD 3D to design over
1300 Japanese plants to date.&nbsp; Additionally, in early 2006, Intergraph acquired Alias Ltd., a leading global provider of piping design automation software, including the popular ISOGEN<sup>&reg;</sup> product.&nbsp; Alias provides complementary critical piping
technology and enhanced expertise in deliverables automation that will allow PP&amp;M to continue delivering productivity improvements to its customers.&nbsp; PP&amp;M utilizes a direct sales distribution model complemented by distributors in certain geographic
areas.</p>

<p></p>

<p>PP&amp;M's business is not considered seasonal.&nbsp; However, the second and fourth quarters historically tend to be higher revenue periods.&nbsp; The Company expects to convert the majority of the segment's 2005 ending backlog to revenue during 2006.&nbsp; This
segment currently has no contracts with the U.S. Government, nor is it dependent on one or a few customers, the loss of which would have a material adverse effect on the division and potentially the Company as a whole.&nbsp; PP&amp;M does not have large working
capital requirements, and does not carry significant inventory or provide extended payment terms on a standard basis.&nbsp;</p>

<p><b><i>SG&amp;I:</i></b>&nbsp; Intergraph's SG&amp;I division provides software and integration services to security, government, and infrastructure organizations around the world.&nbsp; SG&amp;I's geospatially-enabled software allows organizations to protect,
manage, and analyze high-value assets, incidents, and infrastructure.&nbsp; SG&amp;I technology is focused on addressing the specific needs of four core industries: Federal, Military, &amp; Intelligence; Public Safety &amp; Government; Transportation and Commercial
Photogrammetry; and Utilities &amp; Communications.&nbsp; SG&amp;I's software is designed to create a common operational picture that enables organizations to capture, manage, analyze, integrate, and then act on unorganized and complex data.</p>

<p></p>

<p>For more than 25 years, SG&amp;I has been a leading provider of mapping, geospatial information software and cartographic technology solutions.&nbsp; An acknowledged pioneer in the industry, SG&amp;I offers mapping and geospatial software, earth imaging data
acquisition and production technology, and integration services to help solve the complex business challenges of its global customer base, often through a visual representation such as a digital map.&nbsp; SG&amp;I provides complete geospatial technology solutions
for mobile resource management, geospatial intelligence, land information management, geospatial infrastructure management, earth imaging, geospatial resource management, cartographic production, and geospatial data management.&nbsp; In particular, SG&amp;I software
and services address the increased need for geospatial analysis, moving beyond traditional mapping uses and into the essential workflows of its core industries including: global security and public safety agencies utilizing geospatial data for emergency response and
crime analysis; government and intelligence agencies leveraging geospatial technology for change detection analysis of satellite imagery; state departments of transportation using geospatial analysis in intelligent transportation and traffic management for evacuation
planning and preparation for natural disasters; and utility and communications companies utilizing geospatial technology for outage analysis over a certain time period to determine network design issues or weaknesses, as well as optimal allocation of field
workforces.</p>

<p></p>

<p>SG&amp;I solutions are based on its core geospatial technology, which includes the GeoMedia, I/CAD, G/Technology, Terrashare, and ImageScout software applications, as well as the Digital Mapping Camera and ImageStation products.&nbsp; SG&amp;I solutions are
primarily deployed by global security and public safety agencies; local, regional, federal, and national governments; military and intelligence agencies; transportation and commercial remote sensing and photogrammetry organizations; and utilities and communications
companies.&nbsp; SG&amp;I's primary competitors are AutoDesk, CompuDyne, Environmental Systems Research Institute ("ESRI"), GE Smallworld, Leica GeoSystems, Motorola, Northrop Grumman, and TriTech Software Systems.&nbsp; Large system integrators, local software
vendors, and services providers also compete and partner in various industries by offering customized solutions for specific customer requirements.</p>

<p></p>

<p>SG&amp;I's strategy is to differentiate its solutions through its ability to fuse spatial and security technologies into its core markets.&nbsp; For example, SG&amp;I's Command and Control software is often the foundational software technology for the security
systems of high-value assets and critical infrastructure in the global security, public safety, government, and transportation industries.&nbsp; As the core technology for these security systems, Intergraph's software can link incident command, spatial awareness,
intelligent video, employee identification, and a multiple number of sensors into a single system and can integrate with local, state, and federal levels of government to create interoperable, multi-agency, multi-jurisdictional security networks.&nbsp; Through the
integration of these systems, Intergraph delivers the much-needed common operational picture to the intelligence and first-responder communities that enables a faster and more accurate analysis and response to threats on high-value assets and critical
infrastructure.</p>

<p></p>

<p>SG&amp;I capitalizes on Intergraph's global presence and primarily utilizes a direct distribution model to deliver its geospatially-enabled technology solutions to customers in its core industries around the world.&nbsp; This direct business model is complemented
with a targeted indirect channel of distributors and business partner resellers in certain geographic regions.&nbsp; Government agencies worldwide comprise a significant portion of SG&amp;I's customer and revenue base.&nbsp; Though not seasonal, purchases associated
with these customers follow their individual funding authorizations, which can vary between different regions and countries around the world.&nbsp; The U.S. Government is considered a significant and strategic customer for SG&amp;I, with total U.S. Government revenue
accounting for approximately 30% of the division's 2005 customer revenue (and 21% of the Company's consolidated revenue).&nbsp; The Company believes its relationship with the federal government to be good.&nbsp; While it is anticipated that these contracts will
remain in effect through their stated expiration, the contracts are subject to termination and/or non-renewal at the election of the government.&nbsp; Any loss of a significant government contract could have an adverse impact on the segment and potentially the
Company as a whole.</p>

<p></p>

<p>SG&amp;I's primary revenue source is the sale of software and services.&nbsp; SG&amp;I orders tend to be long-term projects and therefore the division expects to complete substantially all of its 2005 ending backlog during 2006 and 2007.&nbsp; While the SG&amp;I
division has a number of milestone payment schedules, it offers no standard extended payment terms.&nbsp; Due to customer demand and interest, the Company also may be asked to develop customized functionality that may extend revenue recognition in accordance with
United States generally accepted accounting principles ("GAAP").&nbsp; The inventories of SG&amp;I consist of deferred long-term project costs arising from percentage-of-completion contracts and component parts inventory for its earth imaging hardware products.&nbsp;
This inventory is purchased in accordance with sales and manufacturing forecasts, with some amount of spare parts for warranty and maintenance purposes.</p>

<p></p>

<p><b><i>Corporate</i></b><b>:&nbsp;</b> The Corporate segment includes oversight activities associated with the offices of the Chief Executive Officer ("CEO"), Chief Operating Officer ("COO"), Chief Financial Officer ("CFO"), Treasurer, Strategic Planning, General
Counsel, Corporate Marketing, the Board of Directors ("Board"), internal and external audit, global IT systems oversight and management, and other costs that are the direct result of Intergraph being a publicly held company.&nbsp;&nbsp; It also includes Teranetix
Europe (a provider of commercial repair and logistics services) and international hardware maintenance, which is a non-core business for the Company.</p>

<p></p>

<p><b>Product Development</b></p>

<p></p>

<p>The Company believes a strong commitment to ongoing product development is essential to success in the markets in which it competes.</p>

<p></p>

<p>Product development expenditures include all costs related to designing new or improving existing products.&nbsp; During the year ended December 31, 2005, the Company expensed $59.7 million (10% of revenues) for product development activities compared to $58.8
million (11% of revenues) in 2004, and $59.0 million (11% of revenues) in 2003.&nbsp; See MD&amp;A and Note 1 of Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements for further discussion of product development expenses, including amounts capitalized and their
recoverability.</p>

<p></p>

<p align="left">The markets in which the Company's business units compete continue to be characterized by technological change, higher-performance and lower-priced product offerings, intense price and performance competition, and development and support of software
standards that result in less specific hardware and software dependencies by customers.&nbsp; The operating results of the Company and its competitors will continue to depend on the ability to accurately anticipate customer requirements and technological trends, and
to rapidly and continuously develop and deliver new products that are competitively priced, offer enhanced performance, and meet customers' requirements for standardization and interoperability.</p>

<p></p>

<p><b>Environmental Affairs</b><br />
</p>

<p>The Company's facilities are subject to numerous laws and regulations designed to protect the environment.&nbsp; In the opinion of the Company, compliance with these laws and regulations has not had, and should not have, a material effect on the capital
expenditures, earnings, or competitive position of the Company.</p>

<p></p>

<p><b>Employees</b></p>

<p></p>

<p>At January 31, 2006, the Company had approximately 3,450 employees.&nbsp; Of these, approximately 1,600 were employed outside the United States.&nbsp; The Company's employees are not subject to collective bargaining agreements, and there have been no work
stoppages due to labor difficulties.&nbsp; Management of the Company believes relations with its employees to be good.</p>

<p></p>

<p><b>International Operations</b></p>

<p></p>

<p>International markets are important to each of the Company's operating segments.&nbsp; Sales outside the United States represented approximately 51%, 50%, and 47% of total revenues in 2005, 2004, and 2003, respectively.&nbsp; Europe and Asia Pacific represented
approximately 31% and 11%, respectively, of total revenues in 2005, compared to 32% and 10%, respectively, in 2004, and 31% and 9%, respectively, in 2003.&nbsp; The Company's operations are subject to and may be adversely affected by a variety of risks inherent in
doing business internationally, such as government policies or restrictions, export control restrictions or enforcement, worldwide political conditions, currency exchange fluctuations, labor laws, and other factors.</p>

<p></p>

<p>Outside the United States, the Company's products are sold and supported through a combination of subsidiaries and distributorships.&nbsp; At January 31, 2006, the Company had approximately 820 employees in Europe, 260 employees in the Asia Pacific region, 150
employees in Canada, and 370 employees in other international locations.</p>

<p></p>

<p>Fluctuations in the value of the U.S. dollar in international markets can have a significant impact on the Company's results of operations.&nbsp; Local currencies are the functional currencies for the Company's Canadian subsidiaries and all but one of the
Company's European subsidiaries.&nbsp; The U.S. dollar is the functional currency for all other international subsidiaries.&nbsp; See Note 1 of Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements for a description of the Company's policy for managing the currency risks
associated with its international operations.</p>

<p></p>

<p>For further discussion of the Company's international operations, see MD&amp;A and Note 15 of Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements.</p>

<p></p>

<p><a name="Item_1A"><b>ITEM 1A.&nbsp; RISK FACTORS</b></a></p>

<p><b></b></p>

<p><b>Risks Relating to Our Business</b></p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Failure to attract, train and retain skilled employees could adversely affect operating results.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The availability of highly trained and skilled technical, professional and management personnel is critical to our future growth and profitability. Competition for scientists, engineers,
technicians, professional and management personnel is intense and competitors aggressively recruit key employees.&nbsp; Because of increased competition for experienced personnel, particularly in highly specialized areas, it can be difficult to meet our employment
needs in a timely manner.&nbsp; Any failure to do so could have an adverse effect on our business.</p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Additionally, many U.S. Government programs require contractors to have security clearances.&nbsp; Security clearances can be difficult and time-consuming to obtain.&nbsp; If our
employees are unable to obtain or retain necessary security clearances, we may not be able to win new business, and our existing customers could terminate their contracts or decide not to renew them.&nbsp; To the extent we cannot obtain or maintain the required
security clearances for our employees working on a particular contract, we may not derive the revenue anticipated from the contract, which could adversely affect our business and results of operations.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Restructuring and cost reduction measures may fail to achieve anticipated results or cause undesirable consequences.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Over the past several years, we have implemented restructuring plans and cost reduction measures, which have included, among other things, the sale of several non-core business units and
assets, significant workforce reductions and consolidation of operations from four to two divisions.&nbsp; Our cost reduction initiatives may also yield unintended consequences, such as attrition beyond our planned reduction in workforce and reduced employee
morale.&nbsp; As a result of these factors, our employees may seek alternate employment.&nbsp; Attrition beyond our planned reduction in workforce could have a material adverse effect on our financial performance. </p>

<p></p>

<p><i>We depend on systems integrators for certain large projects.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Large security projects often involve significant integration services beyond the scale and scope of that typically provided by Intergraph.&nbsp; As a result, in order to be successful
in the sales of our security and public safety products into these markets, we rely on large systems integrators to be the prime contractor to the customer, providing integration services for these large projects.&nbsp; The inability to serve effectively and
economically as a sub-contractor to large systems integrators could therefore limit our ability to grow our security systems revenue and may have a material adverse effect on our results of operations.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>International business operations create additional operational and legal risk.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We derived approximately 47%, 50%, and 51% of our total revenues from customers outside the United States in 2003, 2004 and 2005 respectively.&nbsp; We anticipate that revenues from
customers outside the United States will continue to account for a significant portion of our total revenues for the foreseeable future.&nbsp; Our operations outside the United States are subject to additional risks, including:</p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;changes in or interpretations of foreign law that may adversely affect our ability to sell our products, perform services or repatriate profits to the United States;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;the imposition of tariffs;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;hyperinflation or economic or political instability in foreign countries;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;imposition of limitations on or increase of withholding and other taxes on remittances and other payments by foreign subsidiaries;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;conducting business in places where business practices and customs are unfamiliar and unknown;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;the imposition of restrictive trade policies, including export restrictions;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;worldwide political conditions;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;the imposition of inconsistent laws or regulations;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;the imposition or increase of investment requirements and other restrictions by foreign governments;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;longer collection cycles for account receivables;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;uncertainties relating to foreign laws and legal proceedings;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;having to comply with a variety of U.S. laws, including the Foreign Corrupt Practices Act; &nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;having to comply with U.S. export control regulations and policies that restrict our ability to communicate with non-U.S. employees and supply foreign affiliates and customers;</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Foreign currency fluctuations may affect financial results.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Many of our transactions are denominated in foreign currencies and our foreign subsidiaries generally conduct business in foreign currencies.&nbsp; As a result, we are exposed to
fluctuations in exchange rates. Our risks include the possibility of significant changes in exchange rates and the imposition or modification of foreign exchange controls by either the U.S. or applicable foreign governments.&nbsp; Although we do not currently hedge
against movements in exchange rates for contracts denominated in foreign currencies, we may decide to do so at some point in the future.&nbsp; Any hedging policies implemented by us may not be successful, and the cost of these hedging techniques may have a
significant negative impact on our operating results.&nbsp; Furthermore, we cannot be certain that a significant fluctuation in exchange rates will not have a significant adverse impact on our operating results.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>One or more of our products could become obsolete and adversely impact operating results.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Many of our products are sold in industries that demand advanced technologies.&nbsp; Without timely introduction of new products and enhancements, our products could become
technologically obsolete, in which case our revenue and operating results would suffer.&nbsp; The success of our product offerings depend upon several factors, including our ability to:</p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;accurately anticipate customer needs;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;innovate and develop new technologies and applications; and</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;successfully commercialize new technologies in a timely manner;&nbsp;</p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If we fail at any or all of these, we may invest heavily in research and development of products that do not lead to significant revenues.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>We may be unable to protect our products.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We regard our software as proprietary and rely on a combination of copyright, patent, trademark and trade secret laws, licenses, confidentiality agreements, and software security
measures to protect our proprietary rights.&nbsp; The steps we have taken to protect our proprietary rights may not be adequate to protect our technology from misappropriation or from independently developed competitive technologies that are substantially equivalent
to our technology.&nbsp; The misappropriation of our technology or development of competitive technologies could harm our business and could force us to incur substantial costs in protecting and enforcing our intellectual property rights.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>There is a risk of violating the rights of others, which may prevent us from competing successfully.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We may be sued for infringing the intellectual property rights of others, or may find it necessary to initiate a lawsuit seeking to prove that we do not infringe on the proprietary
rights of others, or that such rights are invalid or unenforceable.&nbsp; Intellectual property litigation is costly, and, even if successful, the cost of such litigation could affect the results of our operations.&nbsp; Furthermore, litigation is time consuming and
could divert management's attention and resources away from our business.&nbsp; We may be found to infringe another's intellectual property rights and may have to pay damages, stop the infringing activity or obtain a license.&nbsp; If we fail to obtain a required
license or are unable to design around a patent, we may be unable to sell some of our products, which could have a material adverse effect on our business, financial condition and results of operations.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Our software is complex and may contain undetected errors.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Complex software products may contain undetected errors or "bugs."&nbsp; Errors or "bugs" may not be detected until after the delivery of the software.&nbsp; These errors could result in
delayed market acceptance or claims against us by customers, which could lead to increased insurance costs.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Liability claims can result in significant expenses and damages.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The sale and implementation of our software and services may entail the risk of product liability and other claims.&nbsp; A failure of such software or services could result in
significant claims against us for damages or for violations of environmental, safety and other laws and regulations.&nbsp; Our agreements with our customers are generally designed to limit our exposure to potential product liability claims.&nbsp; It is possible,
however, that the limitation of liability provisions in our agreements may not be effective as a result of federal, state, local or foreign laws or ordinances or unfavorable judicial decisions.&nbsp; A product liability claim in excess of our insurance coverage or
claim that is outside or exceeds the indemnity protections in our agreements or a recall of one of our products would have to be paid out of our cash reserves and could harm our operating results or financial condition.&nbsp; Some of our software must integrate with
existing computer systems and software of our customers.&nbsp; Some customers may have difficulty in implementing or be unable to integrate these products successfully.&nbsp; As a result, customers may make claims against us relating to the functionality,
performance, or implementation of our products.&nbsp; Such claims may limit our ability to expand our revenues, harm our reputation, and result in customer unwillingness to pay the fees associated with these products and services.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Fixed price contracts may create risk of cost overruns.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;While firm, fixed price contracts allow us to benefit from cost savings and other efficiencies, they may also expose us to potential cost overruns.&nbsp; If estimates used for
calculating a contract price are incorrect, we can incur losses on those contracts.&nbsp; Lower earnings caused by cost overruns could have an adverse effect on our financial results.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Inability to achieve or manage our growth could adversely affect future prospects and results of operations.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We plan to further develop our existing lines of business in our current markets and to expand into new markets.&nbsp; To do this, we will need to enhance our sales and operations, while
managing the restructuring of legacy systems, products and organizations.&nbsp; Our inability to manage and administer these requirements, as needed, may have an adverse effect on our business.</p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We also expect to supplement our internal growth through acquisitions.&nbsp; It is likely that some portion of our planned growth will continue to come from such transactions.&nbsp; We
evaluate potential acquisitions on an ongoing basis.&nbsp; Our acquisitions pose many risks, including:</p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;we may not be able to compete successfully for available acquisition candidates, complete future acquisitions and investments or accurately estimate the financial effect of acquisitions
and investments on our business;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;future acquisitions may require us to issue capital stock or spend significant cash or may result in a decrease in our operating income or operating margins;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;we may have trouble integrating acquired businesses or retaining their personnel or customers;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;acquisitions may disrupt our business and distract our management from other responsibilities.&nbsp;</p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We may not be able to continue to identify attractive acquisitions.&nbsp; If our acquisitions or investments fail or perform poorly, our business could be adversely affected.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Increased financial expectations may not materialize.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The charter of the U.S. Department of Homeland Security has created increased interest in our security and detection products; however, we are not certain whether the level of demand
will continue to be as high as anticipated.&nbsp; We do not know what solutions will be adopted as a result of public safety concerns, and whether our products will be a part of the solution.&nbsp; These factors may adversely impact us and create unpredictability in
revenues and operating results.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Events beyond our control may disrupt operations and harm operating results.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our business may be adversely affected by a war, terrorist attack, third party acts, natural disaster or other catastrophe.&nbsp; A catastrophic event could have a direct negative impact
on us, our customers, the financial markets or the overall economy.&nbsp; It is impossible to fully anticipate and protect against all potential catastrophes.&nbsp; A security breach, criminal act, military action, power or communication failure, flood, hurricane,
severe storm or the like could lead to service interruptions, data losses for customers, disruptions to our operations, or damage to our facilities.&nbsp; Any of these could have a material adverse effect on our business and financial results.&nbsp; In addition, we
may incur costs in repairing any damage beyond our applicable insurance coverage.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Reputation and relationships with governmental entities can affect business operations, financial condition and operating results.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We depend upon the U.S. Government as one of our customers and we believe that the success and development of our business will continue to depend on our successful participation in U.S.
Government contract programs.&nbsp; Total revenue from the U.S. Government was approximately $137.1 million in 2003, $124.9 million in 2004 and $119.6 million in 2005, representing approximately 26% of total revenue in 2003, 23% of total revenue in 2004 and 21% of
total revenue in 2005.&nbsp; We expect to continue to derive a substantial portion of our revenues from work performed under Government contracts.&nbsp; If our reputation or relationship with the U.S. Government, and in particular agencies of the Department of
Defense (DoD) or the U.S. intelligence community, were negatively affected, or if we were suspended or debarred from contracting with government agencies or if the U.S. Government decreased the amount of business with us, our business, financial condition and
operating results could be adversely affected.&nbsp; The U.S. Government also has the right to modify, curtail or terminate the contracts and subcontracts at its convenience.&nbsp; Any decision by the U.S. Government not to exercise contract options or to modify,
curtail or terminate our major programs or contracts could adversely affect our results of operations and financial condition.&nbsp;</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Employee misconduct or our failure to comply with applicable laws or regulations may impact the ability to contract with the U.S. Government.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Misconduct, fraud or other improper activities by our employees or our failure to comply with laws or regulations could have a significant negative impact on our business and
reputation.&nbsp; Such misconduct could include the failure to comply with U.S. Government procurement regulations, regulations regarding the protection of classified information, legislation regarding the pricing of labor and other costs in U.S. Government
contracts, environmental laws and any other applicable laws or regulations. Many of our products are used to manage and protect information relating to national security and other sensitive government functions.&nbsp; A security breach in one of these systems could
prevent us from having access to such critically sensitive systems.&nbsp; Other examples of potential employee misconduct include time card fraud and violations of the Anti-Kickback Act.&nbsp; The precautions we take to prevent and detect these activities may not be
effective, and we could face unknown risks or losses.&nbsp; Our failure to comply with applicable laws or regulations or misconduct by any of our employees could subject us to fines and penalties, loss of security clearance and suspension or debarment from
contracting with the U.S. Government, any of which would adversely affect our business.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Backlog may not be realized as revenue.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Backlog is our estimate of the amount of revenue we expect to realize over the remaining life of definitive contracts and task orders in effect as of the measurement date.&nbsp; Backlog
represents the portion of backlog for which funding currently is appropriated or otherwise authorized and is payable to us upon completion of a specified portion of work, less revenues previously recognized.&nbsp; Certain orders may be cancelled by customers at their
convenience. If we fail to realize revenue amounts included in our backlog, our operating results may be adversely affected.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Contractors or subcontractors who fail to satisfy their obligations to us or the customer can adversely impact our operating results and future business prospects.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;There is a risk that we may have disputes with our subcontractors about the quality and timeliness of work performed by the subcontractor, customer concerns about the subcontractor, our
failure to extend existing task orders or issue new task orders under a subcontract, or our hiring of a subcontractor's personnel.&nbsp; Additionally, if any of our subcontractors fail to deliver and/or perform the agreed-upon products or services, our ability to
fulfill our obligations as a prime contractor may be jeopardized.&nbsp; Subcontractor performance could result in a customer terminating our contract for default.&nbsp; A termination for default could expose us to liability and have an adverse effect on our ability
to compete for future contracts and orders. </p>

<p></p>

<p><i>PP&amp;M revenues include sales to customers in the chemical, petrochemical and petroleum industries, which can impair operating results in an economic downturn.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A large percentage of our PP&amp;M revenues are derived from companies in the chemicals, petrochemicals and petroleum industries.&nbsp; Accordingly, our future success depends, in part,
upon the continued demand from companies in these process-manufacturing industries.&nbsp; The chemicals, petrochemicals and petroleum industries are cyclical and subject to fluctuations in the price of oil.&nbsp; In the past, worldwide economic downturns and pricing
pressures experienced by chemical, petrochemical and petroleum companies have led to consolidations and reorganizations.&nbsp; Such downturns, pricing pressures and restructurings have caused delays and reductions in expenditures by many of these companies and
reduced demand for our products and services.&nbsp; A recurrence of these industry patterns, as well as general domestic and foreign economic conditions and other factors that reduce spending by companies in these industries, could harm our operating results in the
future.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Long sales cycles make it difficult to predict quarterly revenue levels and operating results.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The sales process for our solutions is lengthy and can exceed one year.&nbsp; Accordingly, it is difficult to predict the timing of our revenues.&nbsp; The delay of an order could cause
our quarterly revenues to fall substantially below expectations. Moreover, should our customers desire to purchase integrated solutions in lieu of individual software products, our sales cycle may lengthen, which could increase the likelihood of delays and cause the
effect of a delay to become more pronounced. Delays in sales could cause significant shortfalls in our revenues and operating results for any particular period.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>We face aggressive competition.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our markets are highly competitive.&nbsp; Our PP&amp;M division competes in the plant creation, life cycle management, and operations and maintenance software.&nbsp; Primary competitors
are AVEVA and BSI (an approximately 28% basic or 25% fully diluted, owned affiliate of the Company).&nbsp; Our SG&amp;I division competes with ESRI in the geospatial industry and Motorola Integrated Solutions, Northrop Grumman, Compudyne, TriTech Software Systems, SunGard Data Systems, New
World, Visionair, Interact, and Positron in providing software applications and systems integration services.&nbsp; SG&amp;I also competes with an estimated 3,000 companies in providing IT services to the U.S. Government.</p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Some of our current competitors have significantly greater financial, marketing and other resources than we have.&nbsp; In addition, many of our current competitors have established, and
may in the future continue to establish, cooperative relationships with third parties to improve their product offerings and to increase the availability of their products to the marketplace.&nbsp; The entry of new competitors or alliances into our market could
reduce our market share, require us to lower our prices, or both.&nbsp; Many of these factors are outside our control, and we may not be able to maintain or enhance our competitive position against current and future competitors.</p>

<p></p>

<p><b>Risks Relating to Our Stock</b></p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Fluctuations in quarterly revenues, operating results and cash flow may cause the market price of our common stock to fall.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our revenues, operating results and cash flow have fluctuated in the past and may fluctuate significantly in the future as a result of a variety of factors, many of which are outside of
our control, including:</p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;our customers' purchasing patterns;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;the length of our sales cycle;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;changes in the mix of our license revenues and service revenues;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;the timing of introductions of new solutions and enhancements by us and our competitors;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;weakness in the first quarter of each fiscal year, primarily caused by a slowdown in business in some of our international markets;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;the timing of our investments in new product development;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;failure to estimate costs or control costs under firm fixed price contracts;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;expenses related to acquisitions, mergers or joint ventures;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;decisions by the U.S. Government not to exercise contract options or to modify, curtail or terminate our major programs or contracts;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;instability in the United States and other financial markets and the ongoing and possible escalation of the war in Iraq, other armed hostilities or further acts or threats of terrorism
in the United States or elsewhere;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;a reduction of government funding or delay in the completion of the U.S. Government's budget process;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;changes in our operating expenses; and</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;fluctuating economic conditions, particularly as they affect companies in the chemicals, petrochemicals and petroleum industries.</p>

<p></p>

<p>If, due to one or more of the foregoing factors or an unanticipated cause, our operating results fail to meet the expectations of public market analysts and investors in a future quarter, the market price of our common stock would likely decline.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Broad market and industry factors may adversely affect the market price of our common stock, regardless of our actual operating performance.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Factors that could cause fluctuations in our stock price include, among other things:</p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;actual or anticipated variations in quarterly operating results from historical results or estimates of results prepared by us or financial analysts;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;changes in financial estimates by us, by investors or by any financial analysts who might cover our stock;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;our ability to meet the performance expectations of financial analysts or investors;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;changes in market valuations of other companies in our industry;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;general market and economic conditions;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;announcements by us or our competitors of significant acquisitions, strategic partnerships or divestitures;&nbsp;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;announcements of technological innovations or new products or services by us or our competitors;</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;retention or departures of key personnel; and</p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&bull;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;sales of our common stock, including sales by our directors, officers or principal stockholders.</p>

<p></p>

<p>Fluctuations caused by factors such as these may negatively affect the market price of our common stock.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Sales of a significant number of shares of our common stock by existing shareholders could cause the market price of our common stock to decline.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If our shareholders sell substantial amounts of our common stock, including shares issued upon the exercise of outstanding options, the market price of our common stock may decline.
These sales also might make it more difficult for us to sell equity or equity-related securities in the future at a time and price that we deem appropriate. We are unable to predict the effect that sales may have on the then prevailing market price of our common
stock.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Anti-takeover provisions in our stockholder rights plan and Delaware law could make a third party acquisition of us difficult.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our stockholder rights plan contains provisions that could make it more difficult for a third party to acquire us, even if doing so would be beneficial to our stockholders.&nbsp; We are
also subject to certain provisions of Delaware law that could delay, deter or prevent a change in control.&nbsp; These provisions could limit the price that investors might be willing to pay in the future for shares of our common stock.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>The ultimate amount of cash payment or share settlement in connection with an ASB could materially impact our financial condition and results of operations.</i></p>

<p></p>

<p>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On March 22, 2005, the Company repurchased approximately 5.4 million shares from Goldman Sachs in a private transaction in connection with an ASB program. The shares were repurchased for
an upfront payment of approximately $150.3 million or $27.74 per share, subject to a market price adjustment provision based on the volume weighted average market trading price over the period from May 2, 2005 through March 21, 2006. The Company's volume weighted
average trading price has been approximately $41.10 per share from May 2, 2005 through March 3, 2006.&nbsp; If the Company's share price continues to trade in the range of $36.00 to $39.00 per share throughout the remainder of the ASB period, then the volume weighted
average trading price would be approximately $40.80 to $41.00 per share, and if Intergraph were to settle in cash, the Company would make an additional payment of approximately $70.5 to $72 million to Goldman Sachs, or if Intergraph were to settle in shares, it would
issue approximately 1.8 to 2.0 million shares to Goldman Sachs. The ultimate amount of any additional cash payment or share settlement may vary substantially depending on the final volume weighted average price of the common stock for the applicable period.</p>

<p></p>

<p><a name="Item_2"><b>ITEM 2.&nbsp; PROPERTIES</b></a></p>

<p></p>

<p>The Company's corporate offices and primary product development centers are located in Huntsville, Alabama.&nbsp; The Company's divisions have headquarters located within the Huntsville facilities, and also maintain sales, support, and research and development
facilities throughout the world.&nbsp; Outside of Huntsville, the Company owns office space in the United Kingdom and maintains sales and support facilities in major U.S. cities and other international locations through operating leases.&nbsp; The Company considers
some of its facilities to be in excess of its requirements and may lease or sell excess facilities.</p>

<p></p>

<p><a name="Item_3"><b>ITEM 3. &nbsp;LEGAL PROCEEDINGS</b></a></p>

<p></p>

<p>The Company owns and maintains a number of registered patents and registered and unregistered copyrights, trademarks, and service marks.&nbsp; The patents and copyrights held by the Company are the principal means by which the Company preserves and protects the IP
rights embodied in the Company's products.&nbsp; Similarly, trademark rights held by the Company are used to preserve and protect the reputation of the Company's registered and unregistered trademarks.&nbsp; The Company has sought to protect its IP by engaging in
both licensing and litigation.&nbsp; The Company continues to evaluate various strategies for the protection of its IP in the interest of its shareholders.&nbsp; Such strategies, including litigation, are subject to known and unknown risks and uncertainties.&nbsp;
Adverse developments with respect to the protection of the Company's IP could materially adversely affect the Company's financial condition, results of operations, or prospects.&nbsp; The following is a discussion of the 2005 developments related to the Company's IP
enforcement activities.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</p>

<p></p>

<p><b><i>Clipper System Patents "OEM" Litigation:&nbsp;</i></b> On December 16, 2002, the Company filed a patent infringement action on the Company's Clipper system patents against Dell Inc.<sup>TM</sup> ("Dell"), Gateway, Inc. ("Gateway"), and HP in the U.S.
District Court for the Eastern District of Texas. The Clipper system patents relate to memory management technology.</p>

<p></p>

<p>As previously reported, the Company reached settlements with all three OEM defendants (Dell and Gateway in 2004 and HP in first quarter 2005). Pursuant to the terms of each settlement, Dell, Gateway and HP received a license to the Company's Clipper patents, and
the patent infringement action against each of them was dismissed with prejudice.</p>

<p></p>

<p><b><i>BSI Litigation:&nbsp;</i></b> In December 2002, the Company filed an action in Madison County, Alabama, against BSI (a global software vendor providing architectural, engineering, and construction software solutions) seeking the enforcement of an agreement
and promissory note executed in conjunction with the sale of certain software product lines to BSI in 2000.&nbsp; BSI subsequently filed actions against the Company in Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, and Delaware alleging a breach of the parties' agreement.&nbsp; BSI's
Pennsylvania action was dismissed in March 2003, and BSI's Delaware action was stayed pending the Alabama action.&nbsp; Thereafter, BSI asserted counterclaims in the Alabama action, which were substantially the same as those asserted in Delaware.&nbsp; The Alabama
case was submitted to the Court in 2004, which ruled in favor of Intergraph on both Intergraph's claims as well as BSI's counterclaims.&nbsp; BSI appealed the court decision.&nbsp; In June 2005, the Alabama Supreme Court reversed the trial court decision and remanded
the case for retrial.&nbsp; At this time, no new trial date has been set.</p>

<p><b><i></i></b></p>

<p><b><i>Other Litigation:</i></b><b></b>&nbsp; The Company has other ongoing litigation, none of which is considered to represent a material contingency for the Company at this time; however, any unanticipated adverse developments in any of these proceedings could
materially adversely affect the Company's results of operations, financial condition, or cash flows.</p>

<p></p>

<p><a name="Item_4"><b>ITEM 4.&nbsp; SUBMISSION OF MATTERS TO A VOTE OF SECURITY HOLDERS</b></a><br />
</p>

<p>None.</p>

<p>*&nbsp; Except for the period from February 1998 through August 2001</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>R. Halsey Wise</i> joined the Company in July 2003 as President and CEO and was elected to the Board at that time.&nbsp; Prior to joining Intergraph, he served as CEO, North America of Solution 6 Holdings, Ltd., the largest software company in Australia.&nbsp;
Mr. Wise served as President and CEO of Novient, Inc., a pioneer of internet software solutions for the professional services industry before it was acquired by Solution 6.&nbsp; Prior to joining Novient, Mr. Wise was President and Chief Operating Officer of Computer
Management Sciences, Inc. an IT services company that was later acquired by Computer Associates International, a management software company.&nbsp; At Computer Associates, he was General Manager, North America for Global Professional Services.&nbsp; Prior to that,
Mr. Wise was an investment banker specializing in software and technology services with The Robinson-Humphrey Company.&nbsp; Mr. Wise holds a Masters degree in finance and marketing from the J.L. Kellogg Graduate School of Management at Northwestern University and a
BA degree from the University of Virginia.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Larry J. Laster</i> joined the Company in 1981 and served as Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer from February 1987 through February 1998, at which time he resigned from the Company to serve as Chief Operating Officer of a privately owned
company specializing in the development, sale, and support of business systems for the petroleum distribution and convenience store industries.&nbsp; He rejoined the Company in June 1998 as Chief Financial Officer of Intergraph Public Safety.&nbsp; In September 2001,
Mr. Laster was elected Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer of Intergraph.&nbsp; In January 2005, Mr. Laster announced his intention to resign as CFO as soon as a replacement was hired.&nbsp; In October 2005, Mr. Laster resigned as CFO and was
appointed Senior Vice President and Treasurer.&nbsp; Mr. Laster is a member of the Board of Directors.&nbsp; Mr. Laster holds a bachelor's degree in accounting and is a certified public accountant.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>R. Reid French, Jr.</i> joined the Company in October 2003 as Executive Vice President of Strategic Planning and Corporate Development.&nbsp; In April 2005, Mr. French was appointed Chief Operating Officer.&nbsp; Prior to joining Intergraph, Mr. French served
as Chief Operating Officer, North America for Solution 6 Group, Ltd., Australia's largest software company, directing all regional operations including sales &amp; marketing, product development, services and support.&nbsp; Prior to Solution 6, Mr. French served as a
strategic planner in the Business Planning &amp; Development group for Walt Disney World, a business unit of The Walt Disney Company.&nbsp; Prior to Disney, Mr. French worked in investment banking with The Robinson-Humphrey Company, managing various transactions
within the technology sector.&nbsp; Mr. French began his career as a Fellow of the Thomas J. Watson Foundation.&nbsp; Mr. French holds an MBA degree from the Harvard Business School and a BA degree in economics from Davidson College.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Anthony Colaluca, Jr.</i> joined the Company in October 2005 as Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer.&nbsp; Prior to joining Intergraph, Mr. Colaluca served as Senior Vice President and Chief Financial Officer of Harland Financial Solutions,
Inc., the software and services division of John H. Harland Company, from November 2003 through September 2005.&nbsp; From August 2002 to October 2003, Mr. Colaluca served as Chief Financial Officer for Solution 6 North America, Inc.&nbsp; Mr. Colaluca served as
Chief Financial Officer, Treasurer and Secretary of Novient, Inc. from May 2000 through August 2002.&nbsp; From September 1996 through April 1999, Mr. Colaluca was Vice President and Chief Financial Officer of Computer Management Sciences, Inc., a publicly traded IT
services company that was later acquired by Computer Associates International, a management software company.&nbsp; At Computer Associates, he was Vice President of Finance for the Global Professional Services division.&nbsp; Mr. Colaluca began his career with KPMG
L.L.P. in August 1989 and progressed to Senior Manager when he resigned in September 1996.&nbsp; Mr. Colaluca received a degree in accounting and business administration from State University of New York at Albany and he is a certified public accountant.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Gerhard Sallinger</i> joined the Company in 1985 as a district sales manager in Germany and since then held several positions in the area of sales management.&nbsp; He was elected Vice President Europe of PP&amp;M in 1999 and Executive Vice President Sales and
Marketing of PP&amp;M worldwide in 2001.&nbsp; Mr. Sallinger was appointed President of PP&amp;M in October 2001.&nbsp; Mr. Sallinger holds a degree in chemical engineering from the University of Frankfurt.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Benedict A. Eazzetta</i> joined the Company in 2001 as Director of Business Development for PP&amp;M, and was later promoted to Chief Operating Officer and Executive Vice President of Sales, Marketing &amp; Business Development for PP&amp;M.&nbsp; He was elected
Executive Vice President of the Company and appointed as President of Intergraph Public Safety in 2004.&nbsp; In April 2005, Mr. Eazzetta was appointed to Chief Operating Officer of SG&amp;I and promoted to President of SG&amp;I in January 2006.&nbsp; Prior to
joining Intergraph, Mr. Eazzetta was a co-founder and Vice President of Industria Solutions, an enterprise software company for procurement and materials management, where he managed business and software engineering operations.&nbsp; His background includes 12 years
with Exxon with extensive experience in plant economics improvement programs, technical initiatives and refinery operations.&nbsp; Mr. Eazzetta holds a master's degree in nuclear engineering and a bachelor's degree in mechanical engineering from Georgia Institute of
Technology.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>David Vance Lucas</i> joined the Company in 1994 as staff attorney responsible for corporate, commercial, and IP representation.&nbsp; He was promoted to Senior Counsel in 1997, elected Vice President and General Counsel in 2000, and elected Secretary in
2002.&nbsp; Prior to joining the Company, Mr. Lucas was a partner with the law firm of Johnson, Johnson &amp; Moore.&nbsp; He is admitted to practice before the United States Supreme Court, United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit and the Eleventh
Circuit, as well as both Federal and State Courts in Alabama and Texas.&nbsp; Mr. Lucas holds a bachelor's degree in corporate finance and economics and a juris doctor in law from University of Alabama.</p>

<p></p>

<p><i>Larry T. Miles</i> joined the Company in 1988 as a tax accountant, and since that time has held several positions in the finance and accounting areas.&nbsp; He has served as Vice President of Finance since March 2001. He was elected Corporate Controller and
Chief Accounting Officer in April 2004.&nbsp; Before joining Intergraph, Mr. Miles worked in public accounting for six years.&nbsp; He holds a bachelor's degree in accounting from University of Alabama and is a certified public accountant.</p>
